13:T8294,Abstract algebra studies structures: a set together with one or more operations that obey a short list of rules. Instead of asking "what is $7 \cdot 13$?", it asks "which facts about multiplication follow from the rules alone, so that they hold for integers, for clock arithmetic, for matrices, for symmetries of a molecule, all at once?". That shift pays off in many places: symmetry (crystallography, chemistry, physics), cryptography (RSA and elliptic-curve systems are group theory in disguise), coding theory (error-correcting codes on every phone and disk are built over finite fields), and the classical question of solving polynomial equations, which Galois settled with groups. This series follows the standard senior undergraduate path of Dummit & Foote, Gallian, Artin and Fraleigh: groups, then rings and fields, then Galois theory. Post 00 lays the ground: the proof methods used throughout, sets and functions, equivalence relations, and the arithmetic of the integers up to modular arithmetic, the Chinese remainder theorem and a small but complete RSA example. Every later post uses $\mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$ as its first examples.

> Series map: 00 Integers, Relations & Modular Arithmetic (this post) → 01 Groups & Subgroups → 02 Cosets, Lagrange & Quotient Groups → 03 Homomorphisms, Group Actions & Sylow → 04 Rings, Ideals & Fields → 05 Field Extensions & Galois Theory.

Learning Outcome

- Read and write the four basic proof patterns: direct, contrapositive, contradiction, induction.- Decide whether a function is injective, surjective or bijective, and use the fact that bijections are exactly the invertible functions.- Prove that an equivalence relation on a set is the same thing as a partition of that set.- State and prove the division algorithm from the well-ordering principle.- Run the Euclidean algorithm and the extended Euclidean algorithm by hand, and prove Bézout's identity and Euclid's lemma.- Prove the fundamental theorem of arithmetic (existence and uniqueness).- Compute in $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$, find inverses, and decide which classes are units.- Use Euler's $\varphi$ function, Fermat's little theorem and Euler's theorem to reduce huge powers.- Solve simultaneous congruences with the Chinese remainder theorem.- Carry out a toy RSA key generation, encryption and decryption, and explain why decryption works.

Chapter 1 — The proof toolkit

Abstract algebra is a proof-based subject. Almost every statement is an implication "if $P$ then $Q$", and there are four standard ways to prove one.

Terminology

- Proposition / theorem / lemma / corollary — a proved statement; a lemma is a helper, a corollary follows quickly from a theorem.- Definition — fixes the meaning of a word. Definitions are "if and only if" even when written with "if".- Contrapositive of "$P \Rightarrow Q$" is "not $Q \Rightarrow$ not $P$". It is logically equivalent to the original.- Converse of "$P \Rightarrow Q$" is "$Q \Rightarrow P$". It is a different statement and may be false.- Counterexample — one concrete case that shows a universal statement is false.

Direct proof

Assume $P$, reason forward, reach $Q$.

Claim. The sum of two odd integers is even.

Proof. Odd integers can be written $2a+1$ and $2b+1$ with $a, b \in \mathbb{Z}$. Their sum is $2a + 2b + 2 = 2(a+b+1)$, which is even. $\blacksquare$

Proof by contrapositive

Prove "not $Q \Rightarrow$ not $P$" instead.

Claim. If $n^2$ is even, then $n$ is even.

Proof. Contrapositive: if $n$ is odd then $n^2$ is odd. Write $n = 2k+1$; then $n^2 = 4k^2 + 4k + 1 = 2(2k^2+2k) + 1$, which is odd. $\blacksquare$

Proof by contradiction

Assume the statement is false and derive something impossible.

Claim. $\sqrt{2}$ is irrational.

Proof. Suppose $\sqrt{2} = p/q$ with integers $p, q$, $q \neq 0$, and the fraction in lowest terms. Then $p^2 = 2q^2$, so $p^2$ is even, so $p$ is even by the previous claim. Write $p = 2m$: then $4m^2 = 2q^2$, so $q^2 = 2m^2$ is even and $q$ is even. Both $p$ and $q$ are even, contradicting lowest terms. $\blacksquare$

Proof by induction

To prove a statement $S(n)$ for all integers $n \geq n0$: prove $S(n0)$ (the base case), then prove $S(k) \Rightarrow S(k+1)$ for every $k \geq n0$ (the inductive step). In strong induction the inductive step may assume $S(n0), \dots, S(k)$ all at once.

Claim. For every $n \geq 1$, $1 + 3 + 5 + \dots + (2n-1) = n^2$.

Proof. Base case $n=1$: $1 = 1^2$. Inductive step: if the sum of the first $k$ odd numbers is $k^2$, adding the next one gives $k^2 + (2k+1) = (k+1)^2$. $\blacksquare$

> Watch out: proving the converse is not proving the statement. "If $n$ is even then $n^2$ is even" is true, but it does not prove "if $n^2$ is even then $n$ is even"; that needed its own proof above.

Chapter 2 — Sets, functions and equivalence relations

Terminology

- $\mathbb{N} = \{0, 1, 2, \dots\}$ (some books start at 1; this series includes 0), $\mathbb{Z}$ the integers, $\mathbb{Q}$ the rationals, $\mathbb{R}$ the reals, $\mathbb{C}$ the complex numbers.- $A \times B = \{(a, b) : a \in A, b \in B\}$ is the Cartesian product.- A function $f : A \to B$ assigns to each $a \in A$ exactly one $f(a) \in B$. $A$ is the domain, $B$ the codomain, $f(A)$ the image.- $f$ is injective (one-to-one) if $f(a1) = f(a2)$ implies $a1 = a2$.- $f$ is surjective (onto) if every $b \in B$ equals $f(a)$ for some $a$.- $f$ is bijective if it is both. A bijection from a set to itself is a permutation of the set.- A relation on $A$ is a subset $R \subseteq A \times A$; write $a \sim b$ when $(a, b) \in R$.

Functions and inverses

Examples on $\mathbb{Z}$: $f(x) = 2x$ is injective but not surjective (odd numbers are missed). $g(x) = \lfloor x/2 \rfloor$ is surjective but not injective ($g(0) = g(1) = 0$). $h(x) = x + 5$ is bijective with inverse $x \mapsto x - 5$.

Proposition 2.1. A function $f : A \to B$ has an inverse function $g : B \to A$ (with $g \circ f = \mathrm{id}A$ and $f \circ g = \mathrm{id}B$) if and only if $f$ is bijective.

Proof. If $g$ exists: $f(a1) = f(a2)$ gives $a1 = g(f(a1)) = g(f(a2)) = a2$, so $f$ is injective; and any $b$ equals $f(g(b))$, so $f$ is surjective. Conversely, if $f$ is bijective, each $b$ has exactly one preimage; define $g(b)$ to be it. Then both compositions are identities. $\blacksquare$

Proposition 2.2 (pigeonhole). If $A$ is a finite set and $f : A \to A$ is injective, then $f$ is bijective.

Proof. An injective $f$ sends the $\lvert A\rvert$ elements of $A$ to $\lvert A\rvert$ distinct elements of $A$, so the image is all of $A$. $\blacksquare$

This small fact drives the proofs of Fermat's little theorem below and of Cayley's theorem later in the series. It is false for infinite sets: $x \mapsto 2x$ on $\mathbb{Z}$ is injective but not surjective.

Equivalence relations

A relation $\sim$ on $A$ is an equivalence relation if it is

- reflexive: $a \sim a$ for all $a$;- symmetric: $a \sim b$ implies $b \sim a$;- transitive: $a \sim b$ and $b \sim c$ imply $a \sim c$.

The equivalence class of $a$ is $[a] = \{x \in A : x \sim a\}$.

Examples: equality on any set; "same birthday" on people; "$a - b$ is divisible by $n$" on $\mathbb{Z}$ (Chapter 5); "same determinant" on $2 \times 2$ matrices. Non-examples: $\leq$ on $\mathbb{R}$ is reflexive and transitive but not symmetric; "$\lvert a - b\rvert \leq 1$" on $\mathbb{R}$ is reflexive and symmetric but not transitive ($0 \sim 1$, $1 \sim 2$, but $0 \not\sim 2$).

A partition of $A$ is a collection of non-empty subsets of $A$ such that every element of $A$ lies in exactly one of them.

Theorem 2.3 (equivalence relations = partitions). If $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $A$, its equivalence classes form a partition of $A$. Conversely, every partition $\mathcal{P}$ of $A$ comes from exactly one equivalence relation: $a \sim b$ if and only if $a$ and $b$ lie in the same block of $\mathcal{P}$.

Proof. Each class is non-empty and every $a$ lies in a class, because $a \in [a]$ by reflexivity. It remains to show that two classes are either equal or disjoint. Suppose $c \in [a] \cap [b]$, so $c \sim a$ and $c \sim b$. Take any $x \in [a]$: then $x \sim a$, $a \sim c$ (symmetry), $c \sim b$, so $x \sim b$ by transitivity twice, and $x \in [b]$. Hence $[a] \subseteq [b]$, and by the same argument $[b] \subseteq [a]$. So overlapping classes are equal.

Conversely, given a partition, "same block" is reflexive (every element is in a block), symmetric (obvious), and transitive (if $a, b$ share block $P$ and $b, c$ share block $Q$, then $b \in P \cap Q$, so $P = Q$ because blocks are disjoint). Its classes are exactly the blocks. $\blacksquare$

> Trick: to show two classes are equal it is enough to find one common element. This "one overlap means equal" step is used again for cosets in post 02, where it gives Lagrange's theorem almost for free.

Chapter 3 — Divisibility, gcd and the Euclidean algorithm

Terminology

- $a$ divides $b$, written $a \mid b$, if $b = ak$ for some integer $k$. Every integer divides $0$; $0$ divides only $0$.- A prime is an integer $p \geq 2$ whose only positive divisors are $1$ and $p$. An integer $n \geq 2$ that is not prime is composite.- The greatest common divisor $\gcd(a, b)$ of integers not both zero is the largest integer dividing both. Set $\gcd(0, 0) = 0$.- $a$ and $b$ are coprime (relatively prime) if $\gcd(a, b) = 1$.- Well-ordering principle: every non-empty set of non-negative integers has a least element. It is equivalent to induction and is taken as an axiom about $\mathbb{N}$.

The division algorithm

Theorem 3.1 (division algorithm). For integers $a$ and $b$ with $b > 0$ there are unique integers $q, r$ with

$$a = qb + r, \qquad 0 \leq r < b.$$

Proof. Existence: let $S = \{a - kb : k \in \mathbb{Z}, \ a - kb \geq 0\}$. $S$ is non-empty (take $k = -\lvert a\rvert$; then $a - kb = a + \lvert a\rvert b \geq a + \lvert a\rvert \geq 0$). By well-ordering $S$ has a least element $r = a - qb \geq 0$. If $r \geq b$, then $r - b = a - (q+1)b$ is a smaller element of $S$, a contradiction. So $0 \leq r < b$.

Uniqueness: if $qb + r = q'b + r'$ with both remainders in $[0, b)$, then $b(q - q') = r' - r$ and $\lvert r' - r\rvert < b$. The only multiple of $b$ strictly between $-b$ and $b$ is $0$, so $r = r'$ and $q = q'$. $\blacksquare$

For negative $a$ the remainder is still non-negative: $-17 = (-4) \cdot 5 + 3$, not $(-3) \cdot 5 - 2$.

> Common pitfall: in many programming languages  returns  (C, Java, JavaScript) while Python returns . Mathematics always uses $0 \leq r < b$.

The Euclidean algorithm

Lemma 3.2. If $a = qb + r$, then $\gcd(a, b) = \gcd(b, r)$.

Proof. Any common divisor of $a$ and $b$ divides $r = a - qb$; any common divisor of $b$ and $r$ divides $a = qb + r$. The two pairs have the same common divisors, so the same greatest one. $\blacksquare$

Repeating the division algorithm, the remainders strictly decrease and so reach $0$; the last non-zero remainder is the gcd. For $\gcd(240, 46)$:

$$\begin{aligned}240 &= 5 \cdot 46 + 10 \\46 &= 4 \cdot 10 + 6 \\10 &= 1 \cdot 6 + 4 \\6 &= 1 \cdot 4 + 2 \\4 &= 2 \cdot 2 + 0\end{aligned}$$

So $\gcd(240, 46) = 2$. The number of steps is small: it grows like the number of digits (Lamé's theorem bounds it by five times the number of decimal digits of the smaller number).

Bézout's identity and the extended algorithm

Theorem 3.3 (Bézout). For integers $a, b$ not both zero, there are integers $x, y$ with $ax + by = \gcd(a, b)$. Moreover $\gcd(a, b)$ is the smallest positive integer of the form $ax + by$.

Proof. Let $S = \{ax + by : x, y \in \mathbb{Z}\} \cap \{1, 2, 3, \dots\}$. It is non-empty (it contains $a^2 + b^2 > 0$), so it has a least element $d = ax0 + by0$. Divide: $a = qd + r$ with $0 \leq r < d$. Then $r = a - q(ax0 + by0) = a(1 - qx0) + b(-qy0)$ has the same form, so by minimality $r = 0$ and $d \mid a$. Likewise $d \mid b$. Any common divisor $c$ of $a, b$ divides $ax0 + by0 = d$, so $c \leq d$. Hence $d = \gcd(a, b)$. $\blacksquare$

The proof shows $x, y$ exist but does not find them. The extended Euclidean algorithm does: keep, next to every remainder $ri$, integers $si, ti$ with $ri = 240 si + 46 ti$. Start with $r0 = 240 = 240 \cdot 1 + 46 \cdot 0$ and $r1 = 46 = 240 \cdot 0 + 46 \cdot 1$; each new row is "row two above minus $q$ times row above".

| $i$ | $ri$ | $qi$ | $si$ | $ti$ | check $240 si + 46 ti$ ||---|---|---|---|---|---|| 0 | 240 | — | 1 | 0 | 240 || 1 | 46 | — | 0 | 1 | 46 || 2 | 10 | 5 | 1 | −5 | 240 − 230 = 10 || 3 | 6 | 4 | −4 | 21 | −960 + 966 = 6 || 4 | 4 | 1 | 5 | −26 | 1200 − 1196 = 4 || 5 | 2 | 1 | −9 | 47 | −2160 + 2162 = 2 || 6 | 0 | 2 | — | — | stop |

So $2 = 240 \cdot (-9) + 46 \cdot 47$. The same answer comes from back-substitution, reading the divisions from the bottom up: $2 = 6 - 4 = 6 - (10 - 6) = 2 \cdot 6 - 10 = 2(46 - 4 \cdot 10) - 10 = 2 \cdot 46 - 9 \cdot 10 = 2 \cdot 46 - 9(240 - 5 \cdot 46) = 47 \cdot 46 - 9 \cdot 240$.

> Trick: the $x, y$ are not unique: $(x + 23k, \ y - 120k)$ works for every integer $k$, because $240 \cdot 23 = 46 \cdot 120$. Add a check column like the one above; one wrong sign early ruins every later row.

Euclid's lemma

Theorem 3.4 (Euclid's lemma). If $a \mid bc$ and $\gcd(a, b) = 1$, then $a \mid c$. In particular, if a prime $p$ divides $bc$, then $p \mid b$ or $p \mid c$.

Proof. By Bézout, $ax + by = 1$ for some $x, y$. Multiply by $c$: $c = acx + bcy$. The number $a$ divides $acx$ and divides $bcy$ (because $a \mid bc$), so $a \mid c$. For the prime case: if $p \nmid b$ then $\gcd(p, b) = 1$ (the only positive divisors of $p$ are $1$ and $p$), so $p \mid c$. $\blacksquare$

> Watch out: the prime hypothesis matters. $6 \mid 4 \cdot 9 = 36$ but $6 \nmid 4$ and $6 \nmid 9$.

Chapter 4 — The fundamental theorem of arithmetic

Theorem 4.1 (fundamental theorem of arithmetic). Every integer $n \geq 2$ can be written as a product of primes $n = p1 p2 \cdots pk$, and this factorisation is unique up to the order of the factors.

Proof. Existence, by strong induction on $n$. If $n$ is prime, it is its own factorisation. Otherwise $n = ab$ with $2 \leq a, b < n$; by the induction hypothesis both $a$ and $b$ are products of primes, so $n$ is too.

Uniqueness, by induction on $n$. Suppose $n = p1 \cdots pk = q1 \cdots ql$ with all $pi, qj$ prime. Then $p1$ divides $q1 \cdots ql$, so by Euclid's lemma (applied repeatedly) $p1 \mid qj$ for some $j$. Since $qj$ is prime and $p1 \geq 2$, $p1 = qj$. Reorder so that $j = 1$ and cancel: $p2 \cdots pk = q2 \cdots ql$ is a smaller number (or both sides are empty products equal to $1$), and by induction the remaining primes agree up to order. $\blacksquare$

Grouping equal primes gives the canonical form $n = p1^{e1} \cdots pr^{er}$ with $p1 < \dots < pr$. Examples: $360 = 2^3 \cdot 3^2 \cdot 5$, $1001 = 7 \cdot 11 \cdot 13$, $2026 = 2 \cdot 1013$.

Corollary 4.2 (Euclid). There are infinitely many primes.

Proof. Given primes $p1, \dots, pk$, the number $N = p1 \cdots pk + 1$ has a prime factor $p$ by Theorem 4.1. If $p$ were some $pi$, then $p$ would divide $N - p1 \cdots pk = 1$, which is impossible. So no finite list contains every prime. $\blacksquare$

Uniqueness is not automatic. In the set $\{a + b\sqrt{-5} : a, b \in \mathbb{Z}\}$, $6 = 2 \cdot 3 = (1 + \sqrt{-5})(1 - \sqrt{-5})$ gives two genuinely different factorisations into elements that cannot be split further. Explaining when unique factorisation holds is one of the main themes of ring theory (post 04).

Chapter 5 — Congruences and $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$

Terminology

- Fix an integer $n \geq 1$, the modulus. $a$ is congruent to $b$ modulo $n$, written $a \equiv b \pmod{n}$, if $n \mid a - b$.- The class of $a$ is $[a]n = \{a + kn : k \in \mathbb{Z}\}$, also written $\bar{a}$ or just $a$ when the modulus is clear.- $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$ (also written $\mathbb{Z}n$) is the set of the $n$ classes $[0], [1], \dots, [n-1]$.- A class $[a]$ is a unit if there is $[b]$ with $[a][b] = [1]$. The units form $(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^\times$, also written $U(n)$.- A non-zero class $[a]$ is a zero divisor if $[a][b] = [0]$ for some non-zero $[b]$.

Congruence mod $n$ is an equivalence relation: $n \mid 0$ (reflexive); $n \mid a - b$ implies $n \mid b - a$ (symmetric); $n \mid a - b$ and $n \mid b - c$ imply $n \mid a - c$ (transitive). By Theorem 2.3 its classes partition $\mathbb{Z}$, and by the division algorithm each class contains exactly one of $0, 1, \dots, n-1$, so there are exactly $n$ classes.

Arithmetic on classes

Define $[a] + [b] = [a + b]$ and $[a] \cdot [b] = [ab]$.

Proposition 5.1. These operations are well defined: the result does not depend on which representatives are chosen.

Proof. Suppose $a \equiv a'$ and $b \equiv b'$, say $a' = a + kn$ and $b' = b + ln$. Then $a' + b' = a + b + (k + l)n \equiv a + b$ and $a'b' = ab + (al + bk + kln)n \equiv ab$. $\blacksquare$

"Well defined" is the central worry whenever an operation is defined on classes through representatives. The same check, done for cosets, is what forces the definition of a normal subgroup in post 02.

In practice: reduce early and often. For example $3^{100} \bmod 7$: $3^6 \equiv 1 \pmod 7$ (checked below), and $100 = 6 \cdot 16 + 4$, so $3^{100} = (3^6)^{16} \cdot 3^4 \equiv 3^4 = 81 \equiv 4 \pmod 7$.

Units and zero divisors

In $\mathbb{Z}/12\mathbb{Z}$: $[3][4] = [12] = [0]$, so $[3]$ and $[4]$ are zero divisors. On the other hand $[5][5] = [25] = [1]$, $[7][7] = [49] = [1]$ and $[11][11] = [121] = [1]$. The units are exactly $[1], [5], [7], [11]$, the classes coprime to $12$, and each of them is its own inverse.

Theorem 5.2. $[a]$ is a unit in $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$ if and only if $\gcd(a, n) = 1$. If $\gcd(a, n) = d > 1$ and $n \nmid a$, then $[a]$ is a zero divisor.

Proof. If $\gcd(a, n) = 1$, Bézout gives $ax + ny = 1$, so $ax \equiv 1$ and $[x]$ is the inverse. Conversely if $ab \equiv 1$, then $ab - 1 = kn$, so $ab - kn = 1$ and every common divisor of $a$ and $n$ divides $1$. For the last claim: $a \cdot (n/d) = (a/d) \cdot n \equiv 0$, and $[n/d] \neq [0]$ because $0 < n/d < n$. $\blacksquare$

Corollary 5.3. $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$ has every non-zero class invertible if and only if $n$ is prime.

Theorem 5.2 is also an algorithm: the inverse is the Bézout coefficient. To invert $17$ modulo $43$: $43 = 2 \cdot 17 + 9$, $17 = 1 \cdot 9 + 8$, $9 = 1 \cdot 8 + 1$. Back-substitute: $1 = 9 - 8 = 9 - (17 - 9) = 2 \cdot 9 - 17 = 2(43 - 2 \cdot 17) - 17 = 2 \cdot 43 - 5 \cdot 17$. So $17^{-1} \equiv -5 \equiv 38 \pmod{43}$. Check: $17 \cdot 38 = 646 = 15 \cdot 43 + 1$.

Linear congruences

Theorem 5.4. The congruence $ax \equiv b \pmod n$ has a solution if and only if $d = \gcd(a, n)$ divides $b$. In that case it has exactly $d$ solutions modulo $n$.

Proof sketch. A solution means $ax - ny = b$ for some $y$, and by Bézout the values of $ax - ny$ are exactly the multiples of $d$. If $d \mid b$, divide through: $(a/d)x \equiv b/d \pmod{n/d}$, and $a/d$ is a unit mod $n/d$, so $x$ is unique mod $n/d$. That single class mod $n/d$ splits into $d$ classes mod $n$. $\blacksquare$

Example: $14x \equiv 6 \pmod{20}$. Here $\gcd(14, 20) = 2$ divides $6$. Divide by 2: $7x \equiv 3 \pmod{10}$. Since $7 \cdot 3 = 21 \equiv 1$, $7^{-1} \equiv 3 \pmod{10}$, so $x \equiv 9 \pmod{10}$, giving $x \equiv 9$ or $19 \pmod{20}$. Check: $14 \cdot 9 = 126 = 6 \cdot 20 + 6$ and $14 \cdot 19 = 266 = 13 \cdot 20 + 6$.

> Common pitfall: you may not "divide both sides" of a congruence by a number that is not a unit. $2 \cdot 3 \equiv 2 \cdot 9 \pmod{12}$, but $3 \not\equiv 9 \pmod{12}$. Division by $a$ modulo $n$ is multiplication by $a^{-1}$, which exists only when $\gcd(a, n) = 1$; otherwise the modulus must be divided too, as above.

Chapter 6 — Euler's phi, Fermat and Euler

Euler's phi function

$\varphi(n)$ is the number of integers in $\{1, \dots, n\}$ coprime to $n$; by Theorem 5.2 it is the number of units, $\varphi(n) = \lvert(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^\times\rvert$.

- For a prime $p$: $\varphi(p) = p - 1$.- For a prime power: $\varphi(p^k) = p^k - p^{k-1}$, because the numbers in $\{1, \dots, p^k\}$ not coprime to $p^k$ are exactly the $p^{k-1}$ multiples of $p$.- Multiplicative: if $\gcd(m, n) = 1$ then $\varphi(mn) = \varphi(m)\varphi(n)$. This follows from the Chinese remainder theorem (Chapter 7): $a$ is coprime to $mn$ exactly when it is coprime to both $m$ and $n$, and the CRT bijection matches such $a$ with pairs of units.

Together these give, for $n = p1^{e1} \cdots pr^{er}$,

$$\varphi(n) = n \prod{p \mid n} \left(1 - \frac{1}{p}\right).$$

Examples: $\varphi(12) = 12 \cdot \tfrac12 \cdot \tfrac23 = 4$ (the units $1, 5, 7, 11$); $\varphi(36) = 36 \cdot \tfrac12 \cdot \tfrac23 = 12$; $\varphi(100) = 100 \cdot \tfrac12 \cdot \tfrac45 = 40$; $\varphi(35) = \varphi(5)\varphi(7) = 4 \cdot 6 = 24$.

Fermat's little theorem

Theorem 6.1 (Fermat's little theorem). If $p$ is prime and $p \nmid a$, then $a^{p-1} \equiv 1 \pmod p$. Consequently $a^p \equiv a \pmod p$ for every integer $a$.

Proof. Consider the map $x \mapsto ax$ on the set $R = \{[1], [2], \dots, [p-1]\}$ of non-zero classes mod $p$. It lands in $R$ (a product of two units is a unit, so never $[0]$) and is injective, because $ax \equiv ay$ implies $x \equiv y$ after multiplying by $a^{-1}$. By the pigeonhole Proposition 2.2 it is a bijection, so it just reorders $R$. Multiply all elements of $R$ both ways:

$$(a \cdot 1)(a \cdot 2) \cdots (a \cdot (p-1)) \equiv 1 \cdot 2 \cdots (p-1) \pmod p,$$

that is $a^{p-1} (p-1)! \equiv (p-1)! \pmod p$. Every factor of $(p-1)!$ is a unit, so $(p-1)!$ is a unit and can be cancelled: $a^{p-1} \equiv 1$. For the second statement multiply by $a$; if $p \mid a$ both sides are $0$. $\blacksquare$

Check with $p = 7$, $a = 3$: multiplying $1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6$ by 3 gives $3, 6, 2, 5, 1, 4$ modulo 7, the same six classes in a new order, and $3^6 = 729 = 104 \cdot 7 + 1$.

Euler's theorem

Theorem 6.2 (Euler). If $\gcd(a, n) = 1$, then $a^{\varphi(n)} \equiv 1 \pmod n$.

Proof. Repeat the proof above with the set $U$ of the $\varphi(n)$ units mod $n$ instead of $R$: $x \mapsto ax$ permutes $U$, so $a^{\varphi(n)} \prod{u \in U} u \equiv \prod{u \in U} u$, and the product of units is a unit, so it cancels. $\blacksquare$

Fermat is the case $n = p$. Post 02 re-proves both in one line from Lagrange's theorem, because $(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^\times$ is a group of order $\varphi(n)$ and the order of an element divides the order of the group.

Example: the last digit of $7^{222}$. $\varphi(10) = 4$ and $\gcd(7, 10) = 1$, so $7^4 \equiv 1 \pmod{10}$. Since $222 = 4 \cdot 55 + 2$, $7^{222} \equiv 7^2 = 49 \equiv 9$. The last digit is 9.

> Watch out: Euler's theorem needs $\gcd(a, n) = 1$. $2^{\varphi(4)} = 2^2 = 4 \equiv 0 \pmod 4$, not 1. Also, $\varphi(n)$ is an exponent that always works, not always the smallest one: modulo 12, every unit already satisfies $u^2 \equiv 1$ although $\varphi(12) = 4$.

Chapter 7 — The Chinese remainder theorem

Theorem 7.1 (Chinese remainder theorem). Let $n1, \dots, nk$ be pairwise coprime positive integers and $N = n1 \cdots nk$. For any integers $a1, \dots, ak$ the system

$$x \equiv a1 \pmod{n1}, \quad \dots, \quad x \equiv ak \pmod{nk}$$

has a solution, and any two solutions are congruent modulo $N$.

Proof. Existence: put $Ni = N / ni$. Since $ni$ is coprime to every other $nj$, it is coprime to $Ni$, so there is $Mi$ with $Ni Mi \equiv 1 \pmod{ni}$. Let $x = \sumi ai Ni Mi$. Modulo $nj$, every term with $i \neq j$ vanishes (because $nj \mid Ni$), and the $j$-th term is $aj \cdot 1$. Uniqueness: if $x, x'$ both work, each $ni$ divides $x - x'$; the $ni$ are pairwise coprime, so (by Euclid's lemma, repeatedly) their product $N$ divides $x - x'$. $\blacksquare$

Worked example (Sunzi's classic puzzle). Find $x$ with $x \equiv 2 \pmod 3$, $x \equiv 3 \pmod 5$, $x \equiv 2 \pmod 7$. Here $N = 105$.

| $i$ | $ni$ | $ai$ | $Ni = N/ni$ | $Ni \bmod ni$ | $Mi$ | $ai Ni Mi$ ||---|---|---|---|---|---|---|| 1 | 3 | 2 | 35 | 2 | 2 | 140 || 2 | 5 | 3 | 21 | 1 | 1 | 63 || 3 | 7 | 2 | 15 | 1 | 1 | 30 |

Sum: $140 + 63 + 30 = 233 \equiv 23 \pmod{105}$. Check: $23 = 7 \cdot 3 + 2$, $23 = 4 \cdot 5 + 3$, $23 = 3 \cdot 7 + 2$.

In structural language, the theorem says that the map $\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{Z}/n1\mathbb{Z} \times \dots \times \mathbb{Z}/nk\mathbb{Z}$, $[x] \mapsto ([x]{n1}, \dots, [x]{nk})$, is a bijection that respects addition and multiplication. Both sides have $N$ elements, so it is enough to know the map is injective, which is the uniqueness part. Post 01 meets the additive version as "$\mathbb{Z}/6\mathbb{Z}$ is the same group as $\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z} \times \mathbb{Z}/3\mathbb{Z}$", and post 04 the ring version.

> Common pitfall: the moduli must be pairwise coprime. The system $x \equiv 0 \pmod 4$, $x \equiv 1 \pmod 6$ has no solution: the first says $x$ is even, the second says $x$ is odd.

Chapter 8 — A toy RSA

RSA (Rivest, Shamir and Adleman, 1978) is Euler's theorem put to work. The numbers below are tiny so that every step can be checked by hand; real keys use primes of about 1024 bits or more and a padding scheme, and textbook RSA as shown here is not secure.

Key generation.

1. Pick primes $p = 11$, $q = 17$. The modulus is $n = pq = 187$.2. $\varphi(n) = (p-1)(q-1) = 10 \cdot 16 = 160$.3. Pick a public exponent $e$ coprime to 160: $e = 7$.4. The private exponent is $d = e^{-1} \bmod \varphi(n)$: since $7 \cdot 23 = 161 = 160 + 1$, $d = 23$.

Public key $(n, e) = (187, 7)$; private key $d = 23$.

Encryption of the message $m = 88$ (any integer with $0 \leq m < n$): $c = m^e \bmod n$, computed by repeated squaring so that no number exceeds $186^2$:

$$\begin{aligned}88^2 &= 7744 \equiv 77 \pmod{187} \\88^4 &\equiv 77^2 = 5929 \equiv 132 \\88^7 &= 88^4 \cdot 88^2 \cdot 88 \equiv 132 \cdot 77 \cdot 88 \equiv 66 \cdot 88 = 5808 \equiv 11\end{aligned}$$

So the ciphertext is $c = 11$.

Decryption: $c^d = 11^{23} \bmod 187$. Write $23 = 16 + 4 + 2 + 1$ and square repeatedly: $11^2 = 121$, $11^4 \equiv 121^2 = 14641 \equiv 55$, $11^8 \equiv 55^2 = 3025 \equiv 33$, $11^{16} \equiv 33^2 = 1089 \equiv 154$. Then

$$11^{23} \equiv 154 \cdot 55 \cdot 121 \cdot 11 \equiv 55 \cdot 121 \cdot 11 \equiv 110 \cdot 11 = 1210 \equiv 88 \pmod{187}.$$

The original message comes back.

Why it works. $ed = 1 + k\varphi(n)$ for some $k \geq 0$. If $\gcd(m, n) = 1$, Euler gives $m^{ed} = m \cdot (m^{\varphi(n)})^k \equiv m \pmod n$. (If $m$ shares a factor with $n$, the result still holds: check it modulo $p$ and modulo $q$ separately with Fermat, then combine with the CRT.)

Why it is believed hard to break. Anyone who knows $\varphi(n)$ can compute $d$ with the extended Euclidean algorithm, and $\varphi(n)$ is easy from $p$ and $q$. So the security rests on the difficulty of factoring $n$; no fast classical factoring algorithm is known.

Practice Exercises

1. Prove by induction that $6 \mid n^3 - n$ for every integer $n \geq 0$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

Base: $0^3 - 0 = 0$. Step: $(k+1)^3 - (k+1) = (k^3 - k) + 3k^2 + 3k = (k^3 - k) + 3k(k+1)$. The first term is divisible by 6 by hypothesis; $k(k+1)$ is a product of consecutive integers, hence even, so $3k(k+1)$ is divisible by 6. (A direct proof also works: $n^3 - n = (n-1)n(n+1)$ is a product of three consecutive integers, so it is divisible by 2 and by 3.)

</details>

2. On $\mathbb{R}^2$ define $(x1, y1) \sim (x2, y2)$ if $x1^2 + y1^2 = x2^2 + y2^2$. Show this is an equivalence relation and describe the partition.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

It is "$f(p1) = f(p2)$" for the function $f(x, y) = x^2 + y^2$, and any relation of that form inherits reflexivity, symmetry and transitivity from equality. The classes are the circles centred at the origin, plus the single point $(0,0)$.

</details>

3. Use the extended Euclidean algorithm to find $\gcd(252, 198)$ and integers $x, y$ with $252x + 198y = \gcd(252, 198)$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

$252 = 1 \cdot 198 + 54$, $198 = 3 \cdot 54 + 36$, $54 = 1 \cdot 36 + 18$, $36 = 2 \cdot 18$. So the gcd is 18. Back-substitute: $18 = 54 - 36 = 54 - (198 - 3 \cdot 54) = 4 \cdot 54 - 198 = 4(252 - 198) - 198 = 4 \cdot 252 - 5 \cdot 198$. Check: $1008 - 990 = 18$.

</details>

4. Find all solutions of $14x \equiv 6 \pmod{20}$ by the method of Theorem 5.4, and explain why $14x \equiv 5 \pmod{20}$ has none.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

As in Chapter 5: $x \equiv 9$ or $19 \pmod{20}$. For the second congruence, $\gcd(14, 20) = 2$ does not divide 5: the left side $14x - 20y$ is always even, the right side is odd.

</details>

5. Compute $2^{1000} \bmod 13$ and the last two digits of $3^{2026}$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

By Fermat $2^{12} \equiv 1 \pmod{13}$; $1000 = 12 \cdot 83 + 4$, so $2^{1000} \equiv 2^4 = 16 \equiv 3$. For the last two digits work mod 100: $\varphi(100) = 40$ and $\gcd(3, 100) = 1$, so $3^{40} \equiv 1$; $2026 = 40 \cdot 50 + 26$, so $3^{2026} \equiv 3^{26}$. Now $3^5 = 243 \equiv 43$, $3^{10} \equiv 43^2 = 1849 \equiv 49$, $3^{20} \equiv 49^2 = 2401 \equiv 1$. (The order of 3 mod 100 is actually 20, a divisor of 40.) So $3^{26} \equiv 3^{6} = 729 \equiv 29$. The last two digits are 29.

</details>

6. Solve $x \equiv 3 \pmod 4$, $x \equiv 4 \pmod 9$.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

$N = 36$. $N1 = 9 \equiv 1 \pmod 4$, so $M1 = 1$; $N2 = 4$ and $4 \cdot 7 = 28 \equiv 1 \pmod 9$, so $M2 = 7$. Then $x = 3 \cdot 9 \cdot 1 + 4 \cdot 4 \cdot 7 = 27 + 112 = 139 \equiv 31 \pmod{36}$. Check: $31 = 7 \cdot 4 + 3$ and $31 = 3 \cdot 9 + 4$.

</details>

7. With $p = 5$, $q = 11$ and $e = 3$, find $n$, $\varphi(n)$ and $d$; encrypt $m = 7$ and decrypt the result.

<details><summary>Solution</summary>

$n = 55$, $\varphi(n) = 4 \cdot 10 = 40$, $d = 27$ because $3 \cdot 27 = 81 = 2 \cdot 40 + 1$. Encrypt: $7^3 = 343 = 6 \cdot 55 + 13$, so $c = 13$. Decrypt: $13^2 = 169 \equiv 4$, $13^4 \equiv 16$, $13^8 \equiv 256 \equiv 36$, $13^{16} \equiv 36^2 = 1296 \equiv 31$. With $27 = 16 + 8 + 2 + 1$: $31 \cdot 36 = 1116 \equiv 16$, $16 \cdot 4 = 64 \equiv 9$, $9 \cdot 13 = 117 \equiv 7$. The message 7 comes back.

</details>

Cheat-sheet

| Idea | Statement or method ||---|---|| Equivalence relation | reflexive, symmetric, transitive; classes partition the set || Division algorithm | $a = qb + r$, $0 \leq r < b$, unique || Euclidean algorithm | $\gcd(a, b) = \gcd(b, a \bmod b)$; last non-zero remainder || Bézout | $ax + by = \gcd(a, b)$; gcd is the least positive such value || Extended Euclid | new row = row two above minus $q$ times row above || Euclid's lemma | $p \mid bc \Rightarrow p \mid b$ or $p \mid c$ || FTA | unique factorisation into primes, up to order || $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$ | $n$ classes; $+$ and $\cdot$ well defined || Unit test | $[a]$ invertible iff $\gcd(a, n) = 1$; inverse from Bézout || $ax \equiv b$ | solvable iff $\gcd(a,n) \mid b$; then $\gcd(a,n)$ solutions || Euler phi | $\varphi(n) = n \prod (1 - 1/p)$ over primes $p \mid n$ || Fermat | $a^{p-1} \equiv 1 \pmod p$ if $p \nmid a$ || Euler | $a^{\varphi(n)} \equiv 1 \pmod n$ if $\gcd(a, n) = 1$ || CRT | pairwise coprime moduli: unique solution mod $N = \prod ni$ || RSA | $n = pq$, $ed \equiv 1 \pmod{\varphi(n)}$, $c = m^e$, $m = c^d$ mod $n$ |

What's next

Post 01 takes the pattern seen here (a set, one operation, an identity, inverses) and turns it into the definition of a group. $\mathbb{Z}$ under addition, $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$ under addition and $(\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})^\times$ under multiplication become the first examples, joined by permutations, symmetries of polygons, invertible matrices and the quaternions. The division algorithm reappears in the proof that every subgroup of a cyclic group is cyclic, and $\gcd$ returns in the formula $n / \gcd(k, n)$ for the order of $k$ in $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$.

14:T2bfa,You've indexed (Post 03), tuned queries (Post 04), sized the pool (Post 08), and put metrics in place (Post 14). When everything looks healthy but performance still isn't, the answer is one layer deeper — WiredTiger (the storage engine), the working-set vs cache relationship, document compression, MVCC + locks, and the patterns that produce hot keys + large-document problems. This post covers the in-depth performance knobs that decide whether a well-tuned cluster handles 10k QPS or 100k QPS.

> Series map: 00 Foundations → … → 14 Backup & Ops → 15 Performance Deep (this post) → 16 Atlas → 17 Drivers & Case Studies.

Learning Outcome

- Explain WiredTiger cache + how the working set decides RAM sizing.- Configure + compare snappy / zstd / zlib compression.- Understand document-level locking + MVCC + ticket exhaustion.- Recognise + fix hot-key / large-document / array-growth patterns.- Use GridFS for files bigger than 16 MB.- Diagnose with , , FTDC.

Chapter 1 — WiredTiger cache

WiredTiger is MongoDB's default storage engine (since 3.2). It keeps recently-accessed B-tree pages + index entries in an in-memory cache:

- Default size: .- Tunable: .

Cache holds:

- Indexes pages (priority).- Recently-read document pages.- Active write buffers.

Cache hit rate matters more than absolute RAM. A query touching an index page already in cache is microseconds. Touching one from disk is milliseconds (SSD) or tens of ms (slow disk).

Inspect:

Key fields:

--  (modified, not yet checkpointed)-  — bad sign; app waited for eviction-  — fine-  — fine; happens periodically

If  is climbing fast, the cache is undersized for your working set.

Chapter 2 — Working set sizing — the most important number

The working set is "data + indexes you actually touch frequently". For read-heavy workloads, working set should fit in the WiredTiger cache.

Rough estimate:

You can measure:

-  → , .-  → which indexes are actually used.

If working set > cache:

- Cache thrashes — every query causes evictions.- Queries that should be < 1 ms become 10-50 ms.- Latency p99 spikes.

Fix priority:

1. Drop unused indexes.2. Reduce document size (don't store unused fields; compress text outside Mongo).3. Archive old data (Atlas Online Archive or manual move).4. Scale up to a bigger RAM tier.5. Last resort: shard (Post 11) — distributes working set across multiple machines.

Chapter 3 — Compression

WiredTiger compresses blocks before writing to disk. Three options, configurable per collection or globally:

| Codec | CPU | Ratio | When ||-------|-----|-------|------||  (default) | low | 3× | balanced; default for collections ||  (5.0+) | medium | 4× | better ratio, modest CPU bump; modern default for new collections ||  | high | 5× | archival, cold data, CPU plentiful ||  | none | 1× | rarely useful |

Per-collection at creation:

For most new collections in 2026,  beats snappy: similar speed, better compression → smaller cache footprint → higher hit rate.

Indexes are compressed by prefix compression separately (always on).

> Trick: if your working set barely fits in RAM, switching from snappy → zstd often gives you 25 % more effective cache for free. Test under load before flipping in production.

Chapter 4 — Document-level locking + MVCC

WiredTiger uses multi-version concurrency control (MVCC):

- Readers see a consistent snapshot at their start time.- Writers create new versions; old versions linger until no reader needs them.- Locking is at the document level — different documents in the same collection don't block each other.

Practical implications:

- High concurrency on different documents = fine.- High concurrency on the SAME document = serialised (hot-key problem, next).- Long readers force old versions to linger → memory pressure.

No collection-level lock during ordinary CRUD. DDL (createIndex, drop, collMod) still locks the collection briefly.

Chapter 5 — Hot keys + write hotspots

Symptom: a small subset of documents (one user, one product, one counter) receives most writes. WiredTiger serialises writes per document. The cluster has spare capacity but that one document caps throughput.

Examples:

- A "global stats" doc incremented on every event.- A celebrity user's profile updated by every fan's like.- An auto-increment sequence counter.

Fixes:

1. Sharded counter pattern — split one counter into N shards, increment a random one, sum on read:

2. Move to per-event docs — instead of incrementing a counter, insert one doc per event; aggregate on read. Trade write hotspot for storage + aggregation cost. Use TTL to bound storage.

3. Async aggregation — log raw events; a background job rolls them up periodically (change stream + ).

Chapter 6 — Large-document anti-pattern

Documents have a 16 MB BSON ceiling. Approaching it brings problems even earlier:

- Every update rewrites (copy-on-write) the whole document — slow.- Indexes on fields inside huge docs are inefficient.- Network + WiredTiger cost grows.

Practical limit: keep docs under 1 MB for hot writes.

Causes of bloat:

- Unbounded arrays — comments, followers, history.- Embedded binary data — images, PDFs.- Schema drift — old fields accumulating.

Fixes:

1. Cap arrays: .2. Move to child collection.3. GridFS for large binary (Chapter 7).4. Compress before storing (gzip text), decompress on read.

For files that need to live in MongoDB, GridFS is the safer answer.

Chapter 7 — GridFS

GridFS splits large files into chunks (default 255 KB) stored across two collections ( + ).

Use GridFS when:

- Files > 16 MB.- You want metadata + access control alongside the file in one system.- Replication + sharding of the files for free.

Better alternatives (in 2026):

- S3 (or other object storage) + store URL + metadata in MongoDB. Cheaper, faster, scales further.- GridFS is convenient for small projects; object storage wins at scale.

Chapter 8 — Concurrency tickets (recap from Post 08)

WiredTiger limits concurrent storage-engine operations via read + write tickets (default 128 each).

near 0 = exhaustion → operations queue → latency spikes.

Causes:

- Slow queries holding tickets too long (most common).- Sudden write storm exceeding capacity.- Bad index plans.

Fix order:

1. Find + fix slow queries (Post 04).2. Reduce app-side concurrency (smaller pool).3. Scale up.

Tickets aren't directly tunable in modern versions — they're sized by the storage engine.

Chapter 9 — Long readers + MVCC pressure

A long-running read holds an MVCC snapshot. Old document versions can't be reclaimed until the reader is done. Long readers + heavy writers = memory pressure (cache fills with version chains).

Symptoms:  growing, cache evictions climbing during long-running aggregations.

Mitigations:

- Set  on every user-facing query (Post 02).- Run heavy aggregations on a secondary (with ) to isolate.- Break long aggregations into chunked stages writing to a  target.

Chapter 10 — Index-pages-in-RAM math

A useful sanity check:

If , you can't keep indexes in RAM — queries that should be index-only will spill to disk.

Get sizes:

Add up the ones from  (Post 04) with non-trivial . That's your hot index footprint.

Chapter 11 — FTDC + diagnostic data

MongoDB writes Full-Time Diagnostic Data Capture files to . Continuous metrics, queryable after the fact with tools like  + Atlas's built-in analyzer.

For self-hosted root-cause analysis:

- Capture FTDC during an incident; share with MongoDB support or analyse via OSS tools.- Combine with mongod logs (slow ops, election events, ticket waits).

Atlas: FTDC is collected automatically; the Atlas team can access it for support cases.

Chapter 12 — Sample production tuning checklist

For a new self-hosted production cluster:

- [ ] WiredTiger cache sized to fit working set + headroom.- [ ]  compression for cold-ish collections.- [ ] Indexes audited; unused dropped.- [ ] Hot-counter pattern uses sharded counters.- [ ] No collection-level arrays growing unbounded.- [ ] Large binaries in GridFS or S3, not embedded.- [ ]  on user-facing queries.- [ ] Profiler at .- [ ] FTDC retained for 30 days.- [ ] Prometheus + Grafana dashboard alerting on the Post 14 metrics.- [ ] Restore drill scheduled monthly.

Chapter 13 — Common pitfalls

| Pitfall | Symptom | Fix ||---------|---------|-----|| Working set > RAM | latency spike under load; cache evictions high | scale RAM; drop indexes; archive old data || Hot key | one doc gets all writes; throughput capped | sharded counter pattern || Unbounded array | doc grows toward 16 MB | cap with ; move to child collection || Files in MongoDB | hot writes slow + GridFS overhead | S3 || Default snappy when zstd would fit better | larger on-disk footprint | switch (test first) || Long-running aggregation on primary | cache pressure | run on secondary; or  chunked || Ticket exhaustion | latency spike + tickets near 0 | fix slow queries first; reduce client concurrency || Index not in RAM | predictable slow queries | index pruning; bigger tier || Big update copy-on-write | slow updates on huge docs | shrink the doc; partial updates only of what changed |

15:T55cf,Schema design is the decision with the longest half-life in any system. Indexes can be added, queries can be rewritten, hardware can be replaced — but a wrong key choice or a wrong normalization decision propagates into every query, every migration, and every downstream consumer for years. This post covers the formal part (normal forms, keys, constraints), the pragmatic part (when denormalization is correct, how to model hierarchies and temporal data), the PostgreSQL-specific part (JSONB and when it is the right answer), and the operational part (how to change a schema without locking production out of its own database).

> Series map: 00 Foundations & Setup → 01 SQL Fundamentals & Data Types → 02 Joins, Aggregation & Window Functions → 03 Indexes & the Query Planner → 04 Query Optimization & Statistics → 05 Transactions, MVCC, Isolation & Locking → 06 Schema Design, Normalization & JSONB (this post) → 07 Operations, Backup, Replication & Security.

Learning Outcome

- Decompose a table through 1NF, 2NF, 3NF and BCNF, and state what each step eliminates.- Make an informed, reversible decision to denormalize, and name the cost you accepted.- Choose between natural keys,  identity, UUIDv4 and UUIDv7 with index locality as the deciding factor.- Model one-to-many, many-to-many, inheritance, and hierarchies with the standard patterns and their trade-offs.- Represent temporal data with range types and forbid overlaps with an  constraint.- Apply a clear rule for when a value belongs in JSONB and when it belongs in a real column, and index JSONB correctly.- Run schema migrations with expand/contract so no deployment needs a maintenance window.

Chapter 1 — Normalization, worked

Terminology

- Functional dependency —  means every value of  determines exactly one value of .- Candidate key — a minimal set of columns that functionally determines every other column.- Primary key — the candidate key you chose to designate.- Partial dependency — a non-key column depending on only part of a composite key.- Transitive dependency — a non-key column depending on another non-key column.- Update anomaly — one logical fact stored in many rows, so changing it requires changing many rows and can leave them inconsistent.

Start with the classic denormalized order sheet, one row per line item:

1NF — atomic values, no repeating groups

and  hold lists. Split them into rows; every cell now holds a single value.

1NF eliminates: parsing strings in  clauses, and the impossibility of indexing or constraining an individual product.

2NF — no partial dependencies on a composite key

The key is . But , , , ,  depend on  alone. Split.

2NF eliminates: repeating the customer's email once per line item.

3NF — no transitive dependencies

In , : those depend on the customer, not on the order. In , . Both are non-key columns determining other non-key columns.

3NF eliminates: the update anomaly where changing a supplier's phone number requires touching every order line they ever appeared on.

Note  stays on  even though products have prices. That is not a normalization failure — the price at the time of sale is a genuinely different fact from the current catalogue price. Ask "does this value depend on the key, or on the moment?" before deleting a column that looks redundant.

BCNF — every determinant is a candidate key

3NF still permits an anomaly when a table has overlapping candidate keys. Classic example: a course scheduling table where each course is taught by one instructor, and each instructor teaches one course.

BCNF decomposition:

In practice 3NF is the working target and BCNF violations are rare; they show up mostly where two natural keys overlap.

Chapter 2 — The honest case for denormalization

Normalization optimises for write correctness. It costs joins on read. Denormalize when you can state, in one sentence, which read you are buying and which write cost you are paying.

Defensible denormalizations:

| Pattern | Buys | Costs | Keep correct with ||---------|------|-------|-------------------|| Cached counter () | Removing a  from every page render | Drift if the write path is missed | Trigger, or a scheduled reconciliation job || Copied immutable value () | Historical accuracy, not just speed | Nothing — this is not really denormalization | Nothing needed || Denormalized read table / summary table | A dashboard query that would otherwise join six tables | Staleness, extra storage, refresh cost | Materialized view or incremental refresh (post 04) || Duplicated  on child tables | Row-level security and partition pruning without joins | An extra column to keep consistent | Foreign key on the composite  |

Indefensible ones: duplicating a mutable attribute with no trigger and no reconciliation, or "we might need to join later so let's flatten now". The test is whether you can describe how the copy stays correct. If you cannot, you have not denormalized — you have introduced a bug with a schedule.

> Watch out: this trigger serialises all comment inserts for a single hot post onto one row lock. For a viral post that is a contention bottleneck. The alternative is an append-only delta table summed periodically — a trade of freshness for throughput.

Chapter 3 — Keys: natural, surrogate, and index locality

Terminology

- Natural key — a key drawn from the data itself: ISBN, ISO country code, email address.- Surrogate key — a meaningless generated identifier: a sequence or a UUID.- Index locality — how close together in the btree consecutive inserts land. High locality means new entries pack into the same few rightmost pages.

Natural keys are excellent when the value is genuinely immutable, short, and externally defined —  ISO country codes, currency codes, ISBNs. They are dangerous when the "obviously stable" value turns out to be mutable: email addresses change, national ID formats get reissued, SKUs get renumbered in an acquisition. A natural key that changes forces a cascading update across every referencing table.

Default to a surrogate primary key and a  constraint on the natural key. You get stable references and enforced uniqueness.

bigint identity vs UUIDv4 vs UUIDv7

| |  identity | UUIDv4 | UUIDv7 ||---|---|---|---|| Size | 8 bytes | 16 bytes | 16 bytes || Insert locality in a btree | Perfect — always the rightmost page | Terrible — every insert lands on a random page | Good — time-prefixed, so near-sequential || Generatable client-side | No (needs a round trip or a sequence cache) | Yes | Yes || Leaks information | Yes — row counts and creation order are guessable | No | Creation timestamp only || Merging data from multiple sources | Collides | Safe | Safe |

Index locality is the real issue, not the extra 8 bytes. With random UUIDv4 primary keys, every insert dirties a different btree leaf page. Once the index exceeds the shared buffer cache, each insert becomes a random read plus a random write, write amplification rises through full-page writes in the WAL, and the index fragments. This is the reason UUIDv4 primary keys develop a reputation for "getting slow at scale" — and the reason UUIDv7, which puts a millisecond timestamp in the high bits, fixes most of it while keeping client-side generation.

Practical guidance:

- Single database, ids never leave the system → .- Ids exposed in URLs, generated on the client, or merged across services → UUIDv7.- UUIDv4 → only when unpredictability of ordering is itself a requirement.- Never store a UUID as . The  type is 16 bytes; the text form is 36 plus overhead and compares slower.

Chapter 4 — Modelling patterns

One-to-many

A foreign key on the many side. Decide the delete behaviour explicitly — the default  is a good default precisely because it forces the conversation.

> Common pitfall: PostgreSQL indexes the referenced side (it must be unique) but never the referencing side. A missing index on the child's foreign key column makes every parent delete or update do a sequential scan of the child table, and makes the child hold locks longer.

Many-to-many with a composite primary key

The composite primary key does double duty: it enforces "a tag can only be applied once" and serves as the access path from post to tags. The second index serves tag to posts. Add a surrogate key to a join table only when something else must reference the relationship itself.

Inheritance: single table vs class table

For a hierarchy of related entities — say  with  and  subtypes:

| Approach | Shape | Good | Bad ||----------|-------|------|-----|| Single table | One table, a  discriminator, all subtype columns nullable | One query returns everything; no joins | Cannot enforce  per subtype without  gymnastics; wide sparse rows || Class table |  +  + , each subtype table referencing the parent | Proper constraints per subtype; narrow tables | Every read joins; enforcing "exactly one subtype" needs work || Concrete table |  and  fully independent, no parent | Simplest per-type queries | No way to reference "a vehicle" generically; duplicated columns |

Single table with  constraints is the usual pragmatic answer for two or three subtypes with mostly shared columns:

PostgreSQL's own  table inheritance exists but is largely a legacy feature — it does not propagate unique or foreign key constraints to children. Use the patterns above instead.

Hierarchies: adjacency list, closure table, ltree

| Pattern | Storage | Read a subtree | Move a subtree | Notes ||---------|---------|----------------|----------------|-------|| Adjacency list () | 1 column | Recursive CTE | Update one row | Simple, correct, the default choice || Closure table | One row per ancestor/descendant pair | Single indexed join | Delete + reinsert the subtree's pairs | Fast reads, expensive writes, exact ||  | One  path column |  with a GiST index | Rewrite paths of all descendants | Elegant for shallow, rarely-moved trees |

Start with the adjacency list. Move to a closure table or  only when subtree reads are measurably the bottleneck.

Soft deletes and what they cost

The costs are real and cumulative:

- Every query must remember the filter. One forgotten  is a data leak.- Unique constraints stop working.  now blocks re-registering an address whose owner "deleted" their account.- Foreign keys stop meaning what they say. A child can reference a soft-deleted parent.- The table never shrinks, and indexes carry dead weight forever.

Mitigations, in order of preference:

Option 3 keeps the live table clean, the constraints honest, and the history intact. Prefer it unless the row must remain referenceable.

Chapter 5 — Temporal data and exclusion constraints

Two different time concepts are often conflated:

- Transaction time — when the database learned the fact (, ).- Valid time — when the fact is true in the real world (, ).

A price that takes effect next Monday has a valid time in the future and a transaction time of now. Model them separately when both matter.

Range types make valid-time intervals a first-class value and let the database forbid overlaps outright:

An  constraint is the generalisation of : instead of "no two rows are equal on these columns", it says "no two rows satisfy these operators pairwise".  is exactly .

Useful range operators:  overlaps,  contains,  contained by,  adjacent,  union,  intersection. Always use the half-open  bound convention for time — it makes back-to-back intervals adjacent rather than overlapping, and it composes correctly.

For a full history table, / plus an exclusion constraint on  gives a non-overlapping, gap-tolerant slowly-changing-dimension table that the database itself keeps consistent.

Chapter 6 — JSON vs JSONB

Terminology

-  — stores the exact input text. Preserves whitespace, key order, and duplicate keys. Re-parsed on every access.-  — stores a decomposed binary representation. Loses whitespace, key order, and duplicates (last wins). Parsed once on input; indexable.-  — the SQL/JSON path language, available through  and the  /  operators.

Use .  is appropriate only when you must reproduce the input byte-for-byte, which is an audit-log requirement, not a data-modelling one.

Operators

| Operator | Returns | Example ||----------|---------|---------||  |  |  ||  |  |  ||  |  at a path |  ||  |  at a path |  ||  | boolean, containment |  ||  | boolean, key exists |  ||  /  | any / all keys exist |  ||  | boolean, jsonpath matches |  ||  | boolean, jsonpath predicate |  ||  | set of  |  |

Indexing JSONB

Choosing: if you query a known key with equality or range, use an expression btree index. If you query arbitrary keys with containment, use GIN —  when  is all you need, default  when you also need key-existence operators.

> Common pitfall:  compares text, so  is true. Cast explicitly —  — and make sure your index uses the identical expression, cast included, or it will not be used.

The rule for JSONB vs a real column

Make it a real column when any of these is true:

- You filter, sort, join, or group by it regularly.- It must be , unique, checked, or a foreign key.- Every row has it.- Its type matters (dates, money, booleans).

Make it JSONB when all of these are true:

- The shape is genuinely open-ended or supplied by an external system you do not control.- It is read as a whole blob far more often than it is queried by an inner field.- The set of keys varies by row and enumerating them would produce a wide sparse table.

Typical correct shapes: webhook payloads as received, third-party API responses, per-tenant custom fields, feature flags, sparse product attributes that differ by category, and event payloads whose schema evolves faster than your migration cadence.

The failure mode is "the whole row is one JSONB column". You lose constraints, statistics quality, and type safety, and you gain nothing PostgreSQL was not already better at. If you find yourself writing  on every query, that key wanted to be a column. The hybrid — a normalized core with a JSONB sidecar for genuinely variable attributes — is the design that ages well.

For the document-database perspective on the same modelling questions (embed vs reference, and how those map back to relational tables), see the MongoDB course posts  and . The mapping is close: MongoDB's "embed" corresponds to JSONB or a composite type here, and its "reference" corresponds to a foreign key — with the difference that PostgreSQL will actually enforce the reference.

Chapter 7 — Enum vs lookup table, revisited

| | Enum type | Lookup table | Text + CHECK ||---|---|---|---|| Storage | 4 bytes | Text or a small FK | Text || Add a value |  — cannot run inside a transaction block in older versions, and cannot be used in the same transaction that adds it |  — ordinary DML |  + add — takes a lock and validates || Remove or rename a value | Rename since PostgreSQL 10; removal requires recreating the type |  (FK protects in-use values) | Constraint swap || Attach metadata (label, ordering, flags) | No | Yes | No || Ordering | Declaration order, built in |  column | Alphabetical only |

Rule of thumb: an enum for a closed set that genuinely never changes (, weekday names) and where the 4-byte storage matters. A lookup table for anything a product manager might want to add to, or that needs a human-readable label, an ordering, or extra attributes — which in practice is most status columns.

Chapter 8 — Migration discipline

Every schema change happens against a running system. The rule is that a migration must never require the application and the database to change at the same instant.

Expand / contract

Split every breaking change into phases that are individually backwards-compatible:

1. Expand — add the new structure. Nullable column, new table, new index built . Old code keeps working.2. Backfill — populate the new structure in batches, committing as you go so no single transaction holds a snapshot for hours.3. Dual-write — deploy application code that writes both old and new. Reads still come from old.4. Migrate reads — deploy code that reads from new. Verify.5. Contract — stop writing the old structure, then drop it, once you are certain no rollback will need it.

Renaming a column becomes: add  → backfill → dual-write → switch reads → drop . Five deployments instead of one, and zero downtime at every step.

Adding a NOT NULL column safely

Adding a plain column with a default has been non-rewriting since PostgreSQL 11 — the default is recorded in the catalogue and materialised lazily. That makes this fast even on a huge table:

A volatile default still rewrites the whole table:

For that case, and for adding  to an existing column, use the constraint-first route:

The same  →  two-step works for foreign keys, and it is the only sane way to add one to a large table.

Why a careless ALTER TABLE is dangerous

Most  forms take an  lock — the mode that conflicts with everything, including plain . Two things make that far worse than it sounds:

1. The lock request must wait for existing conflicting locks to be released. One long-running  or an open  session (post 05) delays it indefinitely.2. While it waits, every subsequent query queues behind it. PostgreSQL's lock queue is ordered. A migration blocked for 30 seconds behind one slow report takes the whole table offline for those 30 seconds — for everyone.

The discipline:

Wrap DDL in a retry loop with , and check  for long transactions before you start. Also remember:

-  and  avoid the exclusive lock (at the cost of two table passes and the possibility of leaving an  index behind if they fail — check  and re-run).-  cannot run inside a transaction block, which means most migration frameworks need an explicit "no transaction" flag for that step.- Changing a column's type generally rewrites the table. Widening  to  or to  does not; narrowing does.- Adding a column is cheap; dropping one is also cheap (the column is only marked dropped), but the space is not reclaimed until the rows are rewritten.

16:T29f6,A backup you never restored is a hope, not a backup. This post covers the operational reality of running MongoDB in production: backup mechanisms (, file-system snapshots, Atlas continuous backup), point-in-time recovery (PITR), the slow-query profiler +  + , the server-status metrics worth alerting on, the restore drill that turns hopes into backups, and capacity planning.

> Series map: 00 Foundations → … → 13 Security → 14 Backup & Ops (this post) → 15 Performance Deep → 16 Atlas → 17 Drivers & Case Studies.

Learning Outcome

- Use  /  correctly + their limits.- Take consistent file-system snapshots (LVM, EBS, ZFS).- Configure Atlas continuous backup + point-in-time recovery.- Run a restore drill monthly + automate it.- Use the profiler + slow-query log +  + .- Monitor the metrics that matter + alert thresholds.- Plan capacity by working-set + IOPS + connections.

Chapter 1 — Backup approaches

| Approach | What | Best for ||----------|------|----------|| mongodump / mongorestore | logical export of BSON | small DBs (< 100 GB), dev exports, migrations || File-system snapshot (LVM, EBS, ZFS) | block-level snapshot of  | medium-large DBs; consistent if journal included || Atlas Continuous Backup | Atlas-managed snapshot + oplog tailing | Atlas users; PITR included || Percona Backup for MongoDB | open-source PITR | self-hosted production |

Pick by scale + ops staff + Atlas vs self-hosted.

Chapter 2 — mongodump / mongorestore

Pros: works against any version + cloud; portable file.

Cons:

- Slow for big DBs — serialises every doc through the driver.- Not consistent across collections without  (replica-set source only).- No PITR by itself.

Use for: dev datasets, migrations between accounts/regions, ad-hoc collection-level export.

Consistent dump from a replica set

captures oplog events during the dump;  applies them on restore — gives a consistent snapshot across the whole DB.

Chapter 3 — File-system snapshots

For self-hosted production with bigger volumes, snapshot the data volume:

Steps for consistency:

1. Ensure journaling enabled (default — yes).2. Take the snapshot while  is running (the journal ensures consistency on restore).3. Restore: attach snapshot to new instance, .

For replica sets, snapshot a secondary to avoid impacting primary I/O. To get a strictly-consistent point-in-time, run  on the secondary first → snapshot → .

Throughput: snapshots are fast (block-level); restore time = mount + replication catch-up.

Chapter 4 — Point-in-Time Recovery (PITR)

Snapshots give you "the state as of snapshot time". PITR lets you restore to any second within a retention window — recover from "we accidentally dropped this collection 22 minutes ago".

How it works

- A periodic base snapshot.- Continuous oplog archiving between snapshots.- Restore = base snapshot + replay oplog to the target second.

Atlas

Atlas continuous backup ships PITR by default on dedicated tiers. Restore via UI: pick a point in time, choose target (new cluster, in-place, or download).

Retention configurable (typical: 7-30 days continuous + monthly snapshots for longer retention).

Self-hosted

Use Percona Backup for MongoDB (PBM) or MongoDB Cloud Manager for PITR. Configure base snapshot + oplog archive to S3 / GCS / Azure Blob.

Chapter 5 — Restore drills

A backup is meaningless if you've never tested it. Schedule:

- Monthly: pick a random recent backup; restore to a temporary cluster; verify a small sanity-check query returns expected data. Record RTO (time taken).- Quarterly: full PITR drill — restore a DB to a specific past second.- Annually: tabletop disaster scenario (region down). Walk through your runbook.

Track:

- RTO (Recovery Time Objective) — how long restore takes.- RPO (Recovery Point Objective) — how much data you'd lose (depends on backup frequency + oplog window).

Common surprises in first drills:

- Restored cluster's IAM / users are missing.- App connection string still points at the old cluster.- Restore is slower than expected because IOPS is limited on the target tier.- Indexes rebuild in the background, performance is poor for hours.

Fix during the drill, not during an actual outage.

Chapter 6 — Profiler + slow-query log

Recap from Post 04 — enabling profiler:

Captures every operation taking > 100 ms into . Query for analysis:

Production: keep  configured + ship logs to Datadog / Splunk / Loki for long-term analysis.

Atlas: Performance Advisor + Query Insights surface the same data + suggest indexes.

Chapter 7 — currentOp + killOp

Reactive triage during incidents. Tag every endpoint with  (Post 02) so you can find its ops quickly.

For chronic offenders, find their query shape via profiler + fix the index (Post 04).

Chapter 8 — Server status — metrics that matter

A massive JSON blob. Key metrics worth alerting on:

| Metric | Alert when | Why ||--------|------------|-----||  / max | > 80 % | pool exhaustion incoming ||  rate | growing fast | possible client misuse ||  rate | sudden anomaly | traffic spike or stuck loop ||  vs configured | near max + evictions high | working set > cache (Post 15) ||  | near 0 | ticket exhaustion ||  | sustained high | contention ||  /  | growing per op | indexes not used well || Replication lag | > 60 s | secondary falling behind || Oplog window | < 4 h | resync risk if a secondary falls behind || Disk free | < 20 % | running out || CPU sustained > 80 % | undersized or runaway query |

Atlas dashboards surface all of these out of the box. Self-hosted: Prometheus + the MongoDB Prometheus exporter + Grafana is the standard stack.

Chapter 9 — Capacity planning

Plan four dimensions:

1. Working set vs RAM

The working set = hot indexes + frequently-accessed docs. Must fit in WiredTiger cache (default 50 % of RAM - 1 GB) for fast reads. If it doesn't, every query touches disk → latency 10-100× worse.

Estimate working set: cumulative size of indexes used in hot queries + 5-10 % of frequently-accessed documents. If RAM < working set, scale up.

2. Storage IOPS

Random read IOPS = how many "miss the cache" queries per second you can serve.

- Atlas tiers have provisioned IOPS per disk type.- AWS gp3: configurable up to 16k IOPS per volume.- NVMe instance-store: 100k+ IOPS but ephemeral.

If IOPS is the bottleneck (visible as high disk queue depth + slow queries despite indexes), scale storage tier.

3. CPU

For pipelines + aggregations + many concurrent queries, CPU matters. Aggregation-heavy workloads peg CPU before disk.

4. Connections

App fleet ×  ≤ tier connection limit (Post 08). Plan headroom for failover bursts.

Sizing decision tree

Chapter 10 — Atlas-specific ops tools

| Tool | What ||------|------|| Performance Advisor | suggests indexes from slow queries || Query Insights | groups slow queries by shape with example explains || Real-Time Performance Panel | live ops + metrics || Charts | dashboards on your data || Backup snapshots + PITR | one-click restore || Alerts | configurable on every metric || Online Archive | tier old data to cheap storage || Data Federation | query across Atlas + S3 in one go |

Use it. It saves hours of self-built tooling.

Chapter 11 — Disaster recovery patterns

| Scenario | Strategy ||----------|----------|| Accidental drop | restore from snapshot or PITR to the second before || Region outage | Atlas multi-region cluster auto-fails over to surviving region || Logical corruption (bug wrote bad data for hours) | PITR to before the bad write || Ransomware on self-hosted | offsite, immutable, encrypted backups || Account compromise (Atlas) | MFA + SSO + delete-protected backup snapshots + IAM-only access |

Test these scenarios — RTO + RPO aren't real until you've measured them.

Chapter 12 — Common pitfalls

| Pitfall | Cause | Fix ||---------|-------|-----|| Backup never restored | "we have backups" complacency | monthly restore drill ||  chosen for huge DB | slow + no PITR | use Atlas backup or PBM || Snapshot taken without journal | inconsistent restore | journaling on (default); snapshot while running OK || Lost the connection string after restore | restored cluster has new IDs | scripted restore including app reconfig || Profiler off in production | "performance overhead" myth | enable with , overhead is minor || Metrics not alerted | reactive ops | dashboards + alerts on the table in Ch 8 || Working set > RAM | growth wasn't planned | scale tier; add indexes; archive old data || Atlas tier connection limit hit | client leaks | Post 08 || Restore is super slow | restore tier too small | provision IOPS on restore target || Backups not encrypted at rest | self-hosted pipeline | encrypt the backup bucket |
